# WEEK 7 — Hypothesis Testing I: Probability, Base Rates, Bayes

**Phase:** Automaticity · **Difficulty:** 8/10 · **Time pressure:** moderate

### Cognitive objective

Turn "which explanation is true?" into "**how much should this evidence move me, and what test would move me most?**" You will work with conditional probability, base rates, Bayes' theorem, expected value, and — from Day 3 — confounding, selection bias, and Simpson's paradox. Nearly every Bayesian problem here is a **base-rate trap**: the intuitive answer is wrong for a reason you can name.

**Week-7 rules.**
1. **Attempt every Bayes problem before reading anything about it.** The *intuition* explanations are in the key and are meant to be read only after you have committed an answer (and a guess) — that is when they teach.
2. On each Bayes item, first write your **gut estimate** in one line, *then* calculate. Record the gap: it measures your base-rate intuition.
3. Close every open-ended answer with **"What evidence would change my mind?"**

---

## Toolbox (read once; no answers given here)

**Bayes' theorem**

$$
P(H|E)=\frac{P(E|H)\,P(H)}{P(E)}
$$

with `P(E) = Σ P(E|Hᵢ) P(Hᵢ)` over all hypotheses.

**Natural-frequency method.** Imagine 100,000 people. Split by prior (prevalence), then by test result, and count. Most Bayes errors disappear when you count people instead of multiplying decimals.

**Odds form.** `posterior odds = prior odds × likelihood ratio`, where `LR = P(E|H)/P(E|¬H)`.

**Diagnostic terms.** *Sensitivity* = `P(+|disease)`. *Specificity* = `P(−|no disease)`. *PPV* (precision) = `P(disease|+)`. *NPV* = `P(no disease|−)`.

**Expected value.** `EV = Σ (probability × payoff)`. *EVPI* = expected value with perfect information − best expected value without it.

**Causal vocabulary.** *Confounder:* a common cause of both X and Y. *Mediator:* lies on the path X → M → Y. *Collider:* a common effect of X and Y; conditioning on it creates a spurious association. *Selection bias:* your sample was filtered on something related to the outcome.

---

## Day 1 — Conditional probability and base rates (≈ 55 min)

#### W7D1-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** (a) Two fair dice are rolled; at least one shows a 6. Probability that the sum is 7? (b) A family has two children; at least one is a girl. Probability both are girls? (c) `P(rain) = 0.3`. If it rains, `P(traffic jam) = 0.6`; if not, `0.2`. `P(traffic jam)`?

#### W7D1-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Primary — screening.** *A condition has prevalence 0.5%. A test has sensitivity 90% and specificity 95%.* (a) **Gut estimate** of `P(disease | positive)` before calculating. (b) Calculate it. (c) `P(no disease | negative)`. (d) A second, independent test with the same characteristics also comes back positive. `P(disease | both positive)`? State the independence assumption and when it would fail in practice.

#### W7D1-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Fraud detection.** *1 in 2,000 transactions is fraudulent. The detector flags 98% of frauds and 1% of legitimate transactions.* (a) Among flagged transactions, what fraction are fraudulent? (b) How many flagged transactions must analysts review per fraud caught? (c) What specificity would be needed for at least half of flagged transactions to be fraud?

#### W7D1-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *In an A/B test, variant B has a higher conversion rate but lower revenue per visitor than variant A.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a hypothesis about **mix** and one about **statistical noise**.

#### W7D1-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed: 5 min strict — odds form.** (a) Prior 1%, likelihood ratio 10: posterior? (b) Prior 50%, LR 4: posterior? (c) Prior 20%, LR 0.25: posterior? (d) Prior 10%, three independent pieces of evidence each with LR 3: posterior?

#### W7D1-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
**Novel.** Three cards: one red on both sides, one white on both sides, one red on one side and white on the other. One card is drawn at random and you see a red face. Probability the other face is red?

#### W7D1-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 2 — Bayes with several hypotheses; A/B tests; expected value (≈ 55 min)

#### W7D2-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up — sequential updating.** *Three coins in a bag: fair; 70% heads; 30% heads. One is drawn at random and flipped three times: HHH.* Posterior probability of each coin.

#### W7D2-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Primary — manufacturing.** *Supplier A provides 60% of parts (defect rate 2%); supplier B 40% (defect rate 5%).* (a) `P(defect)`. (b) A part is found defective; `P(from B)`? Gut first. (c) If B supplied only 10% of parts, how does (b) change? (d) In one sentence: why did the answer to (b) not equal `0.05 / (0.02 + 0.05)`?

#### W7D2-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**A/B test.** *Variant A: 20 conversions in 200 visitors. Variant B: 30 in 200.* (a) Compute the pooled two-proportion `z`-statistic. (b) Is the difference significant at the 5% level (two-sided, `|z| > 1.96`)? (c) Roughly how many visitors per arm would be needed to detect a 5-point difference at these conversion levels with ~80% power (use `n ≈ 16·p(1−p)/d²`)?

#### W7D2-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Experimental design.** *Employees who attend optional sales training have 12% higher sales.* Design a study to estimate the **causal** effect. Specify: unit and randomisation, control condition, primary outcome, a guardrail outcome, sample-size logic (qualitative), threats (attrition, spillover, novelty), and a **falsification criterion** stated in advance.

#### W7D2-C2 · IR · 3 pts
**Expected value.** *Project A: 60% chance of +100k, 40% chance of −30k. Project B: certain +40k. Project C: 10% chance of +500k, otherwise −20k.* (a) EV of each. (b) EVPI relative to choosing the best-EV project without information *about A's outcome only* (you can choose A or B, learning A's outcome first). (c) One reason to prefer another project despite the EV ranking.

#### W7D2-D1 · IR · 2 pts
**Novel.** A fair coin lands heads 5 times in a row. (a) `P(tails)` on the next flip? (b) If you were uncertain whether the coin is fair and started from a uniform prior over the heads-probability, what is your predictive probability of heads (Laplace's rule `(k+1)/(n+2)`)? Explain the difference between the two questions.

#### W7D2-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 3 — Causal reasoning and confounding (≈ 55 min)

#### W7D3-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** Classify the variable in **bold** as **confounder / mediator / collider / none**.
(a) Smoking → **tar deposits** → lung cancer.
(b) **Age** affects both income and savings; you study income → savings.
(c) **Admission to an elite programme** depends on academic talent *and* athletic ability; among admitted students, talent and athleticism appear negatively correlated.

#### W7D3-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Simpson's paradox.** Two treatments; success counts by severity:

| | Mild: A | Mild: B | Severe: A | Severe: B |
| - | -: | -: | -: | -: |
| Successes / patients | 81 / 87 | 234 / 270 | 192 / 263 | 55 / 80 |

(a) Success rates for A and B within each group and overall. (b) Explain the reversal. (c) Which treatment would you recommend and **what causal assumption** does your recommendation rest on? (d) What additional information could flip your recommendation?

#### W7D3-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Selection.** *An app shows a "rate us" prompt only to users who completed a 10-day streak; the average rating is 4.6/5.* (a) The flaw. (b) The direction of bias for the *general* user base. (c) A sample design that reduces it.

#### W7D3-C1 · IR · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Customers who use discount coupons spend more per order than those who don't.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a confounder, a reverse-causation hypothesis, and one designed test.

#### W7D3-C2 · HT · 3 pts
*In primary schools, shoe size and reading score correlate at r = 0.9.* (a) The likely confounder. (b) An analysis that tests your explanation. (c) The specific prediction that would **falsify** it.

#### W7D3-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
*A study computes the average return of "all mutual funds" from a database of funds that exist today.* (a) Direction of bias in the reported average. (b) The mechanism.

#### W7D3-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 4 — Falsification and experimental design (≈ 55 min)

#### W7D4-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** For each hypothesis write a **specific, measurable prediction that could fail**:
(a) "The new onboarding flow raises 7-day retention."
(b) "The defect spike is caused by supplier lot #37."
(c) "The sales drop is caused by unusual weather."

#### W7D4-B1 · HT · 5 pts ★
**Primary — full design.** *Test whether free shipping raises orders per visitor without cutting margin.* Specify: (1) unit of randomisation and why; (2) primary metric; (3) guardrail metrics; (4) duration and sample-size logic; (5) the decision rule and the threats to validity (novelty, interference, seasonality). *(1 pt each.)*

#### W7D4-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Multiple comparisons.** *You test 20 independent metrics, each at the 5% significance level, with no real effects.* (a) `P(at least one "significant")`. (b) The Bonferroni-corrected threshold for a 5% family-wise rate. (c) What pre-registration changes.

#### W7D4-C1 · IR · 4 pts
**Technical diagnosis.** *A machine's vibration has risen. Priors: bearing wear 0.5, imbalance 0.3, loose mounting 0.2. A spectrum test finds a strong peak at 1× running speed, which has probability 0.2 under bearing wear, 0.9 under imbalance, 0.4 under loose mounting.* (a) Posterior for each. (b) Which hypothesis has been *most weakened*? (c) Propose the next test that best separates the two leading hypotheses, with the outcome each predicts. (d) What evidence would restore the weakest hypothesis?

#### W7D4-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed: 5 min strict.** (a) `1 − 0.9³` (b) `0.3 × 0.5 + 0.7 × 0.2` (c) EV of a bet: 40% to win 150, 60% to lose 80. (d) Prior odds 1 : 3 against, LR 9 — posterior probability?

#### W7D4-D1 · IR · 2 pts
**Novel — asymmetry.** *A theory predicts an event with probability 0.99; a rival predicts it with probability 0.5; priors are equal.* (a) Posterior for the theory if the event occurs. (b) If the event does **not** occur. (c) Which observation is more informative, and what does that say about falsification?

#### W7D4-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 5 — Integration: evaluation, decisions, tails (≈ 55 min)

#### W7D5-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** *"The test is 99% accurate."* (a) Why can't you compute `P(disease | positive)` from this alone? (b) Take sensitivity = specificity = 99% and prevalence 0.1%: `P(disease | positive)`? (c) Prevalence 10%?

#### W7D5-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Model evaluation.** *A loan model scores 10,000 loans; 2% default (200). It flags 300 as high risk; 120 of those default.* (a) Precision. (b) Recall. (c) Accuracy, and the accuracy of "predict nobody defaults". (d) If a missed default costs 10× a false alarm, compare total cost of the model with "flag nobody".

#### W7D5-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Investment.** *A venture bet of 1 unit: 70% returns 0; 25% returns 2; 5% returns 20.* (a) EV (in units returned). (b) The median outcome. (c) With 20 independent bets, `P(no 20× win)`. What does this say about the *reliability* of EV?

#### W7D5-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Employee-engagement scores rose after the company moved to remote work, but performance ratings fell.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *measurement* hypothesis and a *selection* hypothesis (who left/stayed?).

#### W7D5-C2 · IR · 3 pts
*Two hypotheses about a defect: process drift `H₁` (prior 0.6), bad material lot `H₂` (prior 0.4). Evidence: defects appear on only one of three production lines. `P(E|H₁) = 0.2`, `P(E|H₂) = 0.7`.* (a) Posterior for each. (b) The likelihood ratio. (c) What single observation would move you back toward `H₁`?

#### W7D5-D1 · IR · 2 pts
*You pay 10 to roll a die and receive the square of the face.* (a) EV of the payoff. (b) Net EV. (c) Probability that you lose money on a single play. Would a positive EV alone justify playing?

#### W7D5-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 6 — Weekly challenge (60 min; moderate time pressure)

#### W7D6-1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Security.** *1 in 100,000 passengers is a real threat. The scanner detects 99.9% of threats and falsely alarms on 0.5% of non-threats.* (a) **Gut** `P(threat | alarm)`. (b) Compute it. (c) How many false alarms per real threat detected? (d) Why can't scanner improvements alone fix operational load?

#### W7D6-2 · HT · 3 pts ★
*30% of a city's adults smoke. Among smokers 10% have a condition; among non-smokers 2%.* A randomly chosen person has the condition. (a) `P(smoker)`. (b) What does the *relative risk* (5×) not tell you about this?

#### W7D6-3 · IR · 4 pts ★
**Value of information.** *A project succeeds with probability 0.5: payoff +100k on success, −60k on failure; doing nothing pays 0.* (a) EV of investing. (b) EVPI. (c) A **perfect** test costs 15k: should you buy it? (d) An **80%-accurate** test (80% correct in either direction) costs 15k: should you buy it?

#### W7D6-4 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Yield in a chemical batch process dropped from 92% to 86% after switching a raw-material supplier. Supplier-B lots: 84, 85, 88%; supplier-A lots run in the same weeks: 91, 92%. Plant humidity was also high those weeks.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a design for the most informative next experiment.

#### W7D6-5 · IR · 3 pts
*You can't randomise customers, but you can roll out a loyalty perk store-by-store over 8 weeks.* (a) Describe a **difference-in-differences** design. (b) State the key assumption and how to check it. (c) Name one threat that the design does not remove.

#### W7D6-6 · IR · 1 pts 🪤
Which is more likely in five fair coin flips: `HHHHH` or `HTHHT`?

#### W7D6-7 · HT · 4 pts
*You suspect humidity causes a machine's defects.* Design the **most informative test** for falsifying this claim: what you would vary or observe, the outcome that supports vs. refutes it, the confounders you'd guard against, and **what evidence would change your mind**.

# WEEK 8 — Hypothesis Testing II: Causal Structure, Evidence, and Decisions

**Phase:** Automaticity · **Difficulty:** 8/10 · **Time pressure:** moderate

### Cognitive objective

Move from single-step Bayes to **chains of evidence**, from correlation to **causal structure** (confounders, colliders, selection), and from "is it significant?" to **"what is it worth to find out?"** You will practise likelihood-ratio bookkeeping, causal graphs, model comparison, information value, and calibration of your own confidence.

**Week-8 rules.** (1) Draw the causal graph *before* deciding what to adjust for. (2) For every test you propose, state the outcome you'd expect under each hypothesis. (3) End each open-ended answer with **"What evidence would change my mind?"** (4) Continue writing a gut estimate before each Bayes calculation.

---

## Day 1 — Evidence chains and selection effects (≈ 55 min)

#### W8D1-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** (a) Prior odds 1 : 4 in favour of a hypothesis; two independent pieces of evidence with likelihood ratios 3 and 2. Posterior probability? (b) Prior 50%; two independent pieces of evidence, each with LR 0.5. Posterior? (c) A test has sensitivity 80% and specificity 90%. Give `LR⁺` and `LR⁻`.

#### W8D1-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Primary — Monty Hall family.** (a) There are 100 doors, one prize. You pick one; the host, who **knows** where the prize is, opens 98 empty doors, leaving your door and one other. Probability you win by switching? (b) Three doors; the host does **not** know and opens a door at random; it happens to be empty. Probability you win by switching? (c) Explain in one paragraph why (a) and (b) differ, in terms of what the host's action *tells* you.

#### W8D1-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Regression to the mean.** *Store performance in consecutive quarters has correlation r = 0.6. Stores in the top 10% in Q1 (about 1.75 SD above the mean on average) received a training programme; in Q2 their average is 1.05 SD above the mean.* (a) Confirm the "expected" Q2 value with no training effect. (b) Explain why the drop is not evidence that the training hurt. (c) Design a comparison that would identify the training effect.

#### W8D1-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Customer churn fell after a price decrease, but lifetime value fell too.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *cohort-composition* hypothesis.

#### W8D1-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed: 5 min strict.** (a) `1 − 0.99⁵` (b) `P(H|E)` with prior 0.2, `P(E|H)=0.7`, `P(E|¬H)=0.1` (c) EV of paying 100 for a 25% chance at 400 (d) `P(A|B)` given `P(B|A) = 0.8`, `P(A) = 0.1`, `P(B) = 0.2`.

#### W8D1-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
**Prosecutor's fallacy.** *A DNA profile occurs in 1 in a million people. A city has 5 million people. The suspect matches; there is no other evidence.* (a) Expected number of matching people in the city. (b) Approximate probability the suspect is the source. (c) Why is "1 in a million" the wrong answer?

#### W8D1-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 2 — Causal graphs and identification (≈ 55 min)

#### W8D2-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up.** For each graph, say whether to **adjust** for the variable in bold when estimating the *total* effect of `X` on `Y`.
(a) `Z → X`, `Z → Y`, `X → Y` (adjust for **Z**?)
(b) `X → M → Y` (adjust for **M**?)
(c) `X → C ← Y` (adjust for **C**?)

#### W8D2-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Primary — confounding by indication.** *A hospital cohort of 1,000: 500 high-severity, 500 low-severity. High-severity patients receive the drug 80% of the time; low-severity 20%. Death rates: high severity — 30% with drug, 40% without; low severity — 5% with drug, 10% without.* (a) Observed death rate among treated vs untreated. (b) The **causal** effect if everyone were treated vs no one (standardise to the population's severity mix). (c) Name the bias. (d) What does a randomised trial do about it?

#### W8D2-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Natural experiment.** *A scholarship is awarded to students who score ≥ 70 on an exam.* You compare later earnings of students scoring 69 with those scoring 70. (a) Why can this identify the scholarship effect? (b) The key assumption. (c) A limitation on what it estimates.

#### W8D2-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *A machine-learning model scored 94% accuracy offline but only 86% in production.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include *data leakage*, *distribution shift*, and a *measurement* hypothesis.

#### W8D2-C2 · IR · 3 pts
**Information gain.** *A fault has three possible causes with priors 0.5 (H₁), 0.3 (H₂), 0.2 (H₃). Three deterministic tests: T₁ isolates H₁ vs {H₂, H₃} (cost 10); T₂ isolates H₂ vs {H₁, H₃} (cost 5); T₃ isolates H₃ vs {H₁, H₂} (cost 2).* (a) Prior entropy (bits). (b) Expected information gain of each test. (c) Which test do you run first if you maximise gain **per unit cost**? Which if you maximise gain regardless of cost?

#### W8D2-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
*Store A: average basket 50 from 100 customers. Store B: average basket 80 from 900 customers.* (a) Combined average basket. (b) Why is "65" wrong?

#### W8D2-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 3 — Abduction and model comparison (≈ 55 min)

#### W8D3-A1 · HT · 3 pts
*Sales in Region X are 15% below forecast for April.* For each explanation write **one observation that would differ** from the alternatives: (E1) a competitor opened locally; (E2) the data pipeline dropped some April data for Region X; (E3) unseasonal weather.

#### W8D3-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Model selection with penalty.** *Six data points. Model L: linear, 2 parameters, sum of squared errors (SSE) = 0.15. Model Q: quadratic, 3 parameters, SSE = 0.12.* Use `AIC = n·ln(SSE/n) + 2k`, `n = 6`. (a) AIC of each. (b) Which do you prefer? (c) Why is "Q fits better" not sufficient? (d) What additional data would help most?

#### W8D3-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**Explaining away.** *An alarm rings whenever there is a burglary (probability 0.001 per day) or an earthquake (0.002 per day), independently.* (a) `P(burglary | alarm rang)`. (b) You then learn there was an earthquake. `P(burglary | alarm, earthquake)`? (c) Explain the qualitative principle.

#### W8D3-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Delivery lateness rose 25% after a new routing algorithm was introduced, but customer complaints did not rise.* Six-step template (§0.6).

#### W8D3-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed: 5 min strict.** (a) `1 − 0.7⁴` (b) `0.3 × 200 − 0.7 × 50` (c) prior odds 2 : 5, LR 5 — posterior probability? (d) Prevalence 50%, sensitivity 90%, specificity 80% — `P(D|+)`?

#### W8D3-D1 · IR · 2 pts
**Berkson's paradox.** *Among hospitalised patients, disease A and disease B appear negatively correlated; in the general population they are independent.* Explain how this can happen.

#### W8D3-E · Error analysis (10 min)

## Day 4 — Statistical interpretation and technical diagnosis (≈ 55 min)

#### W8D4-A1 · IR · 3 pts
*A study reports a drug "reduces risk by 50% (from 2% to 1%)".* (a) Absolute risk reduction. (b) Number needed to treat. (c) If baseline risk were 0.2% and relative reduction the same, what is the NNT?

#### W8D4-B1 · IR · 5 pts ★
**Primary — server diagnosis.** Daily data:

| Day | Deploys | Requests (k) | Errors |
| --- | ------: | -----------: | -----: |
| Mon | 2 | 100 | 60 |
| Tue | 0 | 110 | 44 |
| Wed | 3 | 90 | 63 |
| Thu | 1 | 105 | 53 |
| Fri | 4 | 95 | 76 |
| Sat | 0 | 60 | 24 |
| Sun | 0 | 55 | 22 |

(a) Errors per thousand requests each day. (b) Describe the relationship with deploys. (c) A colleague says: "It's just a weekday effect". Which single row argues against that, and why? (d) A different explanation that fits the table. (e) A design that would establish causation.

#### W8D4-B2 · HT · 3 pts
*A poll of 1,000 people finds 52% support for a policy.* (a) 95% margin of error (use `1.96·√(p(1−p)/n)` with `p = 0.5`). (b) Does the poll establish majority support? (c) Sample size needed for a ±2-point margin.

#### W8D4-C1 · IR · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Revenue rose but profit fell.* Channel data (revenue, margin):

| Channel | Q1 revenue | Q1 margin | Q2 revenue | Q2 margin |
| ------- | ---------: | --------: | ---------: | --------: |
| A | 60k | 50% | 55k | 50% |
| B | 40k | 20% | 70k | 10% |

(a) Total revenue and profit in each quarter. (b) Profit change by channel. (c) Three competing hypotheses for B's growth with falling margin; the most informative test; a falsification criterion for each.

#### W8D4-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed: 5 min strict.** (a) `0.8 × 0.75` (b) `P(3 heads in 4 fair flips)` (c) EV: 35% to win 40, 65% to lose 20 (d) Prior 30%, LR 7/3 — posterior probability?

#### W8D4-D1 · IR · 2 pts
*A 95% confidence interval for a mean is `[4.1, 5.9]`.* Why is it wrong to say "there is a 95% probability that the true mean is in [4.1, 5.9]" in the frequentist interpretation? What is the correct reading?

#### W8D4-E · Error analysis (10 min)

## Day 5 — Integration: chains, false discoveries, calibration (≈ 55 min)

#### W8D5-A1 · HT · 3 pts
**Warm-up — chain.** *Prior: 30% that a new feature raises retention.* (a) Pilot 1 gives `LR = 2` in favour. Posterior? (b) Pilot 2 (independent market) gives `LR = 0.6`. Posterior? (c) Pilot 3 gives `LR = 4`. Posterior?

#### W8D5-B1 · HT · 4 pts ★
**Primary — feedback.** *Monthly advertising spend and monthly sales correlate at r = 0.8. The company sets each month's ad budget at 10% of the previous month's sales.* (a) Why is this correlation weak evidence that ads cause sales? (b) Give two alternative causal structures. (c) Design a test using geographic holdouts. (d) State a falsification criterion.

#### W8D5-B2 · HT · 3 pts
**False discoveries.** *1,000 hypotheses tested at α = 5% with power 80%.* (a) If 10% are truly effective, what fraction of "significant" results are real? (b) If only 1% are? (c) Implication for evaluating a surprising claim.

#### W8D5-C1 · IR · 4 pts
**Trade-off.** *A new fraud rule cuts annual fraud losses of 2.0M by 30%, but reduces sales (100M, 20% gross margin) by 3% due to false declines.* (a) Net annual effect. (b) The most uncertain parameter. (c) A test to reduce that uncertainty. (d) A decision rule with a threshold.

#### W8D5-C2 · IR · 3 pts
**Calibration.** *Over 50 forecasts you labelled "80% confident", 34 came true.* (a) Your hit rate. (b) Are you over- or under-confident, by how much? (c) The Brier score of five forecasts each stated at 0.8, with outcomes 1, 1, 0, 1, 1.

#### W8D5-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
*A rare event has a 1-in-a-million chance per person per day, and a city of one million people.* (a) Expected number of people it happens to daily. (b) `P(at least one)` on a given day. (c) Why is "unbelievable coincidence" often expected?

#### W8D5-E · Error analysis (10 min)

## Day 6 — Weekly challenge (60 min; moderate–significant time pressure)

#### W8D6-1 · HT · 4 pts ★
*Prevalence 2%. Test 1: sensitivity 90%, specificity 90%. Confirmatory test 2: sensitivity 95%, specificity 99%. A person is positive on both (independent given true status).* (a) `P(D)` after test 1 only. (b) After both. (c) Does the order of testing change the final posterior? (d) Why might independence fail?

#### W8D6-2 · HT · 3 pts ★
*Among promoted employees at a firm, skill and networking ability are negatively correlated.* Explain via a causal graph, and say what would happen if you sampled all employees instead.

#### W8D6-3 · IR · 4 pts ★
**Power and peeking.** *Baseline conversion is 5%. You want to detect a +0.5-point lift with ~80% power at 5% significance (`n ≈ 16·p(1−p)/d²` per arm). Each arm receives 2,000 visitors/day.* (a) Sample size per arm. (b) Duration. (c) Why does stopping at the first `p < 0.05` inflate false positives? (d) One design that allows early stopping.

#### W8D6-4 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world.** *Customer complaints fell 20% while churn rose 10%.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *measurement* hypothesis and a *survivorship* hypothesis.

#### W8D6-5 · IR · 3 pts
*Three analysts independently say "buy". Prior probability the asset is undervalued: 20%; each "buy" has LR 2.* (a) Posterior if independent. (b) Posterior if all three read the same report (perfect correlation). (c) What single question determines which is closer to the truth?

#### W8D6-6 · IR · 1 pts 🪤
In a room of 23 people, what is the approximate probability that at least two share a birthday?

#### W8D6-7 · HT · 4 pts
*A vendor claims: "Our hiring AI is unbiased because it never sees gender."* (a) The flaw. (b) Two mechanisms by which bias can arise anyway. (c) A test of the claim, with the outcome that would **falsify** "unbiased". (d) What evidence would change your mind?

# WEEK 9 — Processing Efficiency and Novelty I

**Phase:** Speed (accuracy-gated) · **Difficulty:** 8–9/10 · **Time pressure:** significant

### Cognitive objective

Increase the rate of **correct** reasoning. You will (1) build a triage habit (first pass / second pass), (2) practise estimation and chunking so that routine sub-steps become automatic, (3) meet *unfamiliar structures* (new operators, new number systems, new notations) where no template exists, and (4) learn to detect **adversarial patterns** where the obvious rule is misleading.

**Speed protocol (all timed blocks this week).**
1. **First pass:** work in order; skip anything that stalls you for > 60–75 seconds; mark it `↷`.
2. **Second pass:** return to skipped items until time is up.
3. **Untimed pass:** after the clock, continue without pressure; mark corrected answers with `U`.
4. **Accuracy gate.** If your accuracy on a timed block is < 75%, record **speed credit = 0** for that block and *widen the next block's time by 25%*. Do not "try harder to go faster".

**Efficiency** = correct answers ÷ minutes. Track it in the scorecard; also record how many errors were `S` (only wrong under the clock) vs. genuine reasoning errors.

---

## Day 1 — Sequence sprints, chunking, estimation (≈ 55 min)

#### W9D1-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**Sprint 1 (6 min).** Next term.
(a) `5, 8, 14, 26, 50, ?`
(b) `2, 5, 10, 17, 26, ?`
(c) `7, 9, 13, 21, 37, ?`
(d) `A, C, F, J, O, ?`
(e) `1, 3, 7, 13, 21, 31, ?`
(f) `64, 32, 24, 12, 4, ?`

#### W9D1-B1 · PE · 8 pts
**Sprint 2 (9 min).** (a) `17 × 23` (b) Which is larger, `3/7` or `4/9`? (c) `2¹³` (d) `15% of 3,400` (e) `√2809` (f) `1/12 + 1/4` (g) `12 × 12 − 11 × 13` (h) `999²`.

#### W9D1-B2 · PD · 2 pts 🪤
**Regime change.** `2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 9, 8, 7, ?` (a) Give the next term under the rule that the sequence has *just* followed. (b) State what a careless solver would answer and which observation you'd need to be sure.

#### W9D1-C1 · WM · 3 pts
**Cover-and-reveal (chunking).** `A3  F7  K2  P9  T4  X6`. (a) Which letter is paired with 9? (b) Sum of the numbers paired with letters A–M. (c) The number paired with the letter *before* X in the list.

#### W9D1-D1 · IR · 3 pts
**Novel — estimation (target 8 min; tolerance ±30% for a, b; ±5 for c).** (a) Seconds in a year. (b) Heartbeats in 80 years at 70 bpm. (c) Digits in `100!` (use Stirling: `n! ≈ √(2πn)(n/e)ⁿ`, and `log₁₀ e ≈ 0.4343`).

#### W9D1-E · Error analysis (10 min) — for every miss, tag **S** (wrong only under time) or a reasoning code.

---

## Day 2 — Speed–accuracy calibration (≈ 55 min)

#### W9D2-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**Sprint (6 min).** (a) All A are B; no B is C. Can any A be C? (b) `X > Y`, `Z < Y`, `W > X`: who is smallest? (c) Five people each shake hands with each other once: handshakes? (d) The angle between the hands at 3:30. (e) The 50th digit after the decimal point of `0.123123123…` (f) The smallest non-negative `n` with `3n + 2` divisible by 7.

#### W9D2-B1 · PE · 8 pts ★
**Calibration set (10 min strict).** Mark each answer with a confidence 1–5.
(1) `4, 7, 12, 19, 28, ?`
(2) Two dice: probability of **exactly one** 6.
(3) 3 cats catch 3 mice in 3 minutes. How many cats to catch 100 mice in 100 minutes?
(4) A 3×3×3 cube is painted on the outside and cut into 27 unit cubes. How many have **exactly two** painted faces?
(5) `AB, BD, CF, DH, ?`
(6) Which is larger: `0.999¹⁰⁰⁰` or `1/3`?
(7) Number of pairs among 12 people.
(8) `7⁴`.

#### W9D2-C1 · PD · 4 pts
**Recognise vs. infer.** (a) `1, 11, 21, 1211, 111221, ?` (b) `3, 1, 4, 1, 5, 9, 2, 6, ?` (c) `1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, ?` (d) For each of (a)–(c): could a solver with **no prior knowledge of the sequence** infer it from the terms alone? What would they need? *(1 pt each for (a)–(c); 1 pt for (d).)*

#### W9D2-C2 · HT · 4 pts
**Real-world (8 min).** *A support team's ticket backlog keeps growing despite two new hires.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *queueing/capacity* hypothesis and an *inflow* hypothesis.

#### W9D2-D1 · IR · 2 pts 🪤
(a) What is the angle between the hands of a clock at 3:15? (b) How many times do the hour and minute hands overlap in 12 hours?

## Day 3 — Estimation and value of information (≈ 55 min)

#### W9D3-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**Estimation sprint (6 min; ±30%).** (a) Minutes in a 2,000-hour work year. (b) Bits in one gigabyte (2³⁰ bytes). (c) Words in a 300-page novel (state your words-per-page). (d) Litres in a 25 m × 10 m × 2 m pool. (e) Days in one million seconds. (f) 0.1% of 4.5 million.

#### W9D3-B1 · IR · 5 pts ★
**Value of a test (12 min).** *A launch pays +500k if the product succeeds (probability 0.4) and −300k if it fails; not launching pays 0. A market test costs 40k; it predicts "success" for 90% of products that will succeed and for 20% of products that will fail.* (a) EV of launching without testing. (b) EV of the policy "test, then launch only if positive", net of the test cost. (c) EVPI. (d) Is the test worth buying? By how much? (e) At what test cost would you be indifferent?

#### W9D3-B2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed 5 min.** (a) Probability of two aces from a 52-card deck without replacement (as a fraction). (b) `P(sum of two dice ≥ 10)`. (c) `P(at least one head in 3 flips)`. (d) Expected number of rolls of a fair die to get the first 6.

#### W9D3-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Ambiguous problem.** *Daily active users are up 8% while sessions per user are down 10%.* List at least three interpretations (including a metric-definition change), the additional data that resolves each, and state which single cut of the data you'd request first. End with: *what evidence would change my mind?*

#### W9D3-C2 · IR · 3 pts
**Sequential choice.** *Ten candidates arrive in random order; you can accept only the current one, no going back. Strategy: reject the first 3, then accept the first candidate better than all seen so far.* (a) Probability of choosing the best (the formula is `(r/n)·Σ₍ₖ₌ᵣ₊₁₎ⁿ 1/(k−1)`). (b) What rule of thumb does this suggest for large `n`? (c) The exploration–exploitation logic in one sentence.

#### W9D3-D1 · IR · 2 pts
**Novel — calendar.** Suppose today is Thursday, 24 September 2026. (a) What weekday is it 100 days from now? (b) What date?

#### W9D3-E · Error analysis (10 min)

## Day 4 — Unfamiliar structures (≈ 55 min)

#### W9D4-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**New operator sprint (6 min).** Define `a ⊕ b = a·b − a − b`.
(a) `3 ⊕ 4` (b) `5 ⊕ 2` (c) Is there an element `e` with `a ⊕ e = a` for all `a`? (d) Solve `2 ⊕ x = 6`. (e) Is `⊕` commutative? (f) Compare `(3 ⊕ 4) ⊕ 2` and `3 ⊕ (4 ⊕ 2)`; is `⊕` associative?

#### W9D4-B1 · AB · 4 pts ★
**Alien arithmetic.** In a number system with digits `0, 1, 2` and place values `1, 3, 9, 27, 81, …`: (a) Value of `121`. (b) Value of `2012`. (c) Write 100 in this system. (d) A rule for deciding if a number written this way is even, using its digits.

#### W9D4-B2 · PD · 2 pts
**Unfamiliar matrix.** Each cell is a 3-bit string; in every row **and** every column, the third entry is the bitwise XOR of the first two.

```
101  011  110
110  101  011
011   ?    ?
```

Find the two missing cells.

#### W9D4-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Ambiguous evidence.** *An initial study (n = 50) found a significant effect (d = 0.6, p = 0.03); a replication (n = 500) found d = 0.15, p = 0.001.* Give three explanations for the discrepancy, say which is most probable and why, and name the data that would discriminate.

#### W9D4-C2 · PE · 4 pts
**Timed 4 min.** (a) `1 + 2 + … + 40` (b) `2⁰ + 2¹ + … + 2⁹` (c) Number of positive factors of 36. (d) `7!/5!`.

#### W9D4-D1 · IR · 2 pts
**Novel.** On an 8×8 chessboard: (a) how many rectangles (of any size, sides along grid lines) in total? (b) how many are squares?

#### W9D4-E · Error analysis (10 min)

**Timed 5 min.** (a) `84 ÷ 12` (b) `16 × 25` (c) `25%` of `25%` (as a percentage) (d) Three consecutive integers sum to 72: the largest? (e) `1 + 3 + 5 + … + 21`.

`1, 2, 4, 7, 11, 16, 22, 29, 38, 46` — one term is inconsistent with the simplest rule. (a) Which, and what should it be? (b) State the rule.

(b) Fraction of good units wrongly rejected overall. (c) The reduction factor in defect rate.

# WEEK 10 — Processing Efficiency and Novelty II

**Phase:** Speed and novelty (accuracy-gated) · **Difficulty:** 8–9/10 · **Time pressure:** significant

### Cognitive objective

Push the rate of **correct** reasoning further while the material stays unfamiliar. This week you will (1) automate modular arithmetic, counting and small-algorithm sub-steps so working memory is free for the hard part, (2) infer unfamiliar functions and notations from a few examples, (3) practise *deciding what to test next* (information value, cost-ordering, value of perfect information), and (4) keep the accuracy gate honest under real time pressure.

**Speed protocol (same as Week 9).** First pass in order, skip after 60–75 s (`↷`); second pass on skipped items; untimed pass after the clock (mark corrections `U`). **Accuracy gate:** timed-block accuracy < 75% ⇒ speed credit = 0 and widen the next block by 25%.

**Time budget note.** Sprint blocks are deliberately slightly too long to finish perfectly. Finishing everything correctly is *not* the goal; correct answers per minute is.

---

## Day 1 — Arithmetic fluency, modular thinking, small structures (≈ 55 min)

#### W10D1-A1 · PE · 8 pts
**Sprint 1 (6 min).** (a) `12 × 13 + 2` (b) `99 × 101` (c) `(5/6)³` (d) `11 × 12` (e) `1/2 − 1/6` (f) `1/8` as a percentage (g) A group's handshakes (everyone shakes everyone's hand once) total 105. How many people? (h) Mean of `15, 20, 25, 30`.

#### W10D1-B1 · PE · 8 pts
**Modular sprint (8 min).** (a) `3¹⁰⁰ mod 7` (b) the last two digits of `7²⁰²⁶` (c) solve `5x ≡ 3 (mod 11)` for `0 ≤ x ≤ 10` (d) how many integers from 1 to 1000 leave remainder 3 when divided by 7? *(2 pts each.)*

#### W10D1-C1 · WM · 4 pts
**Cover-and-reveal (grid).** The 3×3 grid `G` has rows `100 / 110 / 001` (1 = filled). Apply, in order: **rotate 90° clockwise**, then **flip left–right**. (a) Write the result (3 rows). (b) How many cells differ between `G` and the result? (c) Name a single simple operation equivalent to the two-step sequence. (d) Applying that operation twice returns what?

#### W10D1-C2 · WM · 3 pts
**Merge in your head.** Two sorted lists are merged into one sorted list: `L1 = 1, 5, 7, 10` and `L2 = 2, 6, 9`. (a) The 4th element of the merged list. (b) The 6th. (c) The median of the merged list.

#### W10D1-D1 · IR · 4 pts
**Novel — payback estimate (target 8 min; ±10% on (a)–(c)).** *A firm handles 1,000,000 support tickets a year. A new tool saves 5 minutes per ticket. Loaded labour cost is 200 baht/hour. The tool costs 3,000,000 baht.* (a) Hours saved per year. (b) Value per year. (c) Payback period in months. (d) Name the assumption that most threatens the estimate.

#### W10D1-E · Error analysis (10 min) — tag **S** (wrong only under time) or a reasoning code for every miss.

## Day 2 — Function inference, permutations, reliability, testing order (≈ 55 min)

#### W10D2-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**Function-inference sprint (7 min).** Give the requested value; each has a unique intended answer.
(a) `f(1)=3, f(2)=7, f(3)=13, f(4)=21`. Find `f(5)`.
(b) `1, 7, 19, ?` (centred hexagonal numbers).
(c) `1, 3, 7, 15, 31, ?` (next term).
(d) `3 ☆ 4 = 25`, `2 ☆ 5 = 29`, `1 ☆ 1 = 2`. Find `1 ☆ 3`.
(e) A machine maps `12 → 3`, `25 → 7`, `40 → 4`, `99 → 18`. What does `41` map to?
(f) `3 ⊗ 2 = 8`, `4 ⊗ 3 = 15`, `5 ⊗ 4 = 24`. Find `6 ⊗ 5`.

#### W10D2-A2 · PE · 5 pts
**Sprint (4 min).** (a) `7!` (b) `C(8,3)` (c) `(1/2)³` as a decimal (d) `45 × 22` (e) `lcm(8, 9)`.

#### W10D2-B1 · WM · 4 pts
**Permutation tracking (cover-and-reveal).** A permutation σ sends `1→2, 2→4, 3→6, 4→1, 5→3, 6→5`. (a) Write σ as disjoint cycles. (b) Its order (smallest `k > 0` with σᵏ = identity). (c) `σ¹⁰⁰(1)`. (d) A permutation has disjoint cycles of lengths 2, 3 and 5. What is its order?

#### W10D2-B2 · IR · 4 pts
**Reliability.** *A system works if (X or Y) works **and** Z works. X, Y, Z fail independently; reliabilities are X = 0.90, Y = 0.80, Z = 0.95.* (a) System reliability. (b) You can upgrade **one** component to 0.99. Which gives the biggest gain? (c) The resulting system reliability. (d) Explain why "upgrade the weakest component" fails here.

#### W10D2-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**What to check first.** *A fault has three possible causes: H1 (prior 0.5, check cost 20), H2 (0.3, cost 10), H3 (0.2, cost 5). You check causes one at a time until you find it; the last remaining cause need not be checked because it is certain by elimination.* (a) The order that minimises expected cost. (b) That expected cost. (c) The expected cost of checking in order of prior probability (H1, H2, H3). (d) Why "check the most likely cause first" is not optimal.

#### W10D2-D1 · IR · 3 pts
**Novel notation.** Prefix (Polish) notation writes the operator before its operands: `+ 2 3` means `2 + 3`. (a) Evaluate `+ * 3 4 − 10 5`. (b) Evaluate `− / 20 4 * 5 4`. (c) Postfix writes the operator after: evaluate `3 4 + 2 *`.

## Day 3 — Chained evidence, growth, counting (≈ 55 min)

#### W10D3-A1 · PE · 6 pts
**Sprint (6 min).** (a) `C(9,4)` (b) remainder of `2¹⁰⁰` divided by 3 (c) `1/2 + 1/4 + 1/8 + 1/16 + 1/32` (d) `12 × 91` (e) which is largest: `2³⁰`, `3²⁰`, `5¹³`? (f) `111²`.

#### W10D3-B1 · HT · 5 pts ★
**Two tests, one patient.** *Prior probability of a condition is 5%. Test A: sensitivity 80%, specificity 90%. Test B: sensitivity 70%, specificity 95%. Given the condition status, the tests are independent.* (a) The positive likelihood ratio of each test. (b) Probability of the condition after a positive A. (c) After positive A **and** positive B. (d) The sensitivity and specificity of the rule "both positive". (e) 🪤 If both tests actually react to the *same* biomarker, which of your numbers is wrong and in which direction?

#### W10D3-B2 · AB · 4 pts
**Stairs.** *You climb a staircase of 10 steps.* (a) Ways to climb it taking 1 or 2 steps at a time. (b) Ways taking 1, 2 or 3 steps at a time. *(2 pts each; state the recurrence you used.)*

#### W10D3-C1 · IR · 3 pts
**Scale estimation.** *A company receives 2,000,000 resumes a year; a human screener spends 5 minutes on each; one full-time employee (FTE) works 2,000 hours a year.* (a) FTEs needed to screen everything. (b) A tool that halves screening time saves how many FTEs? (c) Name one hidden cost the FTE saving ignores.

#### W10D3-D1 · HT · 3 pts 🪤
*A family has two children. At least one is a boy born on a Tuesday.* (a) The probability that both are boys. (b) Explain why the answer is not 1/2 and not 1/3.

## Day 4 — Information value and ambiguity (≈ 55 min)

#### W10D4-A1 · PE · 5 pts
**Sprint (5 min).** (a) `13²` (b) `log₂ 256` (c) `lcm(6, 10)` (d) `11 × 13` (e) `6! ÷ 2`.

#### W10D4-B1 · HT · 4 pts
**Which test is more informative?** *Eight equally likely candidate causes. Test X splits them 4 / 4 (positive for four, negative for four). Test Y splits them 2 / 6.* (a) The information (bits) an outcome of X yields **on average**. (b) Same for Y. (c) Which test do you run first and why? (d) Is Y ever the better choice? Give a condition.

#### W10D4-B2 · IR · 4 pts
**Value of perfect information.** *You must buy 100,000 units from one supplier. Supplier Y costs 103 per unit for certain. Supplier X costs 100 per unit with probability 1/3 and 109 per unit with probability 2/3.* (a) Expected cost per unit for X. (b) Best choice without further information. (c) Expected cost per unit if you could learn X's price before choosing. (d) The most you should pay (in total) for that information.

#### W10D4-C1 · AB · 4 pts
**A tournament with no winner.** *Five teams play a round robin (each pair once, no draws). Every team finishes with exactly two wins.* (a) How many games were played? (b) Is there a single "best" team? (c) Construct an explicit result table that achieves this. (d) Find three teams A, B, C with A beat B, B beat C, C beat A.

#### W10D4-D1 · HT · 3 pts
**Ambiguity in a metric.** *A subscription service reports "5% monthly churn".* (a) Annual churn if 5% of current customers leave each month (no new customers considered). (b) Explain why the naive answer 60% is wrong. (c) Name **two** different definitions of "monthly churn" that could both be reported as "5%", and how you'd check which one is meant.

#### W10D5-B1 · IR · 4 pts
**Retail case.** *Online orders: 50,000 a month at 190 baht contribution margin. Offline orders: 30,000 a month at 120 baht. A pickup incentive moves 5,000 online orders a month to offline pickup; each moved order earns the offline margin and also costs 220 baht of incentive.* (a) Baseline monthly contribution (millions). (b) Net monthly change from the incentive. (c) The contribution after the change. (d) Name two effects **not** in these numbers that could reverse the sign.

The clues are independent given the culprit.* (a) Posterior odds A : B. (b) Probability A did it. (c) What would make the independence assumption fail?

## Day 6 — Weekly challenge (≈ 75 min, one sitting)

*Items 1–7 ≈ 55 min; item 8 ≈ 15 min. Use the speed protocol.*

**Cover-and-reveal.** A queue starts empty. Operations: `enq(4)`, `enq(6)`, `enq(9)`, `deq`, `enq(8)`, `deq`. (a) The queue now (front first). (b) The sum of dequeued values. (c) Then `enq(1)` and *rotate* (move the front element to the back). What is at the front?

#### W10D6-4 · IR · 3 pts
**Little's law.** *A clinic waiting room holds 36 people on average while 72 people arrive per hour (steady state).* (a) Average waiting time in minutes. (b) If the average stay were 40 minutes at 72 arrivals per hour, the average number present. (c) State the condition under which the law applies.

#### W10D6-5 · IR · 4 pts
**Screening decision (cost units).** *Screen 1,000 people. Prevalence 1%. Test sensitivity 95%, specificity 99%. Testing costs 1.2 units per person; every positive result triggers a follow-up costing 50 units; a missed case costs 500 units. Without screening, every case is missed.* (a) Expected total cost with screening. (b) Without screening. (c) The missed-case cost at which the two are equal (to the nearest unit). (d) One thing this model leaves out.

#### W10D6-6 · PE · 5 pts
**Sprint (5 min).** (a) `7 × 6 × 5 × 4` (b) Probability of exactly one head in three fair flips (c) The largest prime below 60 (d) `1 + 2 + … + 9` (e) Ordered pairs of distinct items from 10.

#### W10D6-7 · IR · 1 pt 🪤
In a race you overtake the runner who is in **second place**. What place are you in now?

#### W10D6-8 · HT · 4 pts
*A city's bike-share ridership fell 15% in the month after a fare rise; a councillor concludes the fare rise "killed" ridership.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *seasonality* hypothesis and a *substitution* hypothesis, and name one comparison that would separate them from the fare effect.

# WEEK 11 — Integrated Reasoning I

**Phase:** Integration · **Difficulty:** 9/10 · **Time pressure:** moderate to high

### Cognitive objective

Combine all five skills on multi-step problems in unfamiliar domains: business cases, technical diagnosis, statistical interpretation, algorithmic reasoning and causal inference. Each day has one domain. The warm-up keeps your arithmetic sharp; the primary and secondary blocks are *multi-constraint* problems where the setup, not the calculation, is the hard part; the novel block is a structure you have not met.

**Working rules.**
1. Before computing, write the **representation** (variables, constraints, what is being asked) in one or two lines.
2. Before finishing, write **what evidence would change your mind** for every conclusion.
3. The accuracy gate applies to every timed block (< 75% ⇒ speed credit 0).
4. ★ items carry double points.

---

## Day 1 — Business and unit economics (≈ 55 min)

#### W11D1-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** (a) A price rises 15%, then 20%. Overall change? (b) A price falls 20%, then rises 25%. Overall change? (c) A product has a 30% margin **on price**. What is the markup **on cost**? (d) A quantity grows 10% a year from 100. In how many whole years does it first exceed 200?

#### W11D1-B1 · IR · 6 pts ★
**Subscription economics (12 min).** *Average revenue per user is 300 baht/month, gross margin 70%, monthly churn 4% (constant), customer acquisition cost (CAC) 1,200 baht.* (a) Customer lifetime value (LTV = monthly gross profit ÷ churn). (b) LTV ÷ CAC. (c) Months to recover CAC. (d) LTV if churn rises to 6%. (e) The churn at which LTV equals CAC. (f) Which assumption in the LTV formula is most likely to be false?

#### W11D1-C1 · HT · 4 pts
**Pricing test (8 min).** *At price 10, a shop sold 1,000 units in week 1. At price 12, it sold 850 units in week 2. Unit cost is 6.* (a) Profit in each week. (b) The implied price elasticity (%ΔQ ÷ %ΔP, using simple percentage changes from week 1). (c) Give **two** reasons the comparison is not a clean test. (d) Design a test that would be.

#### W11D1-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Novel — production planning (12 min).** *A workshop makes P (profit 30 per unit) and Q (profit 20). P needs 2 machine-hours and 1 labour-hour; Q needs 1 machine-hour and 1 labour-hour. Available: 100 machine-hours, 80 labour-hours. Market demand for Q is at most 50 units. Fractional units are allowed.* (a) The optimal plan. (b) Its profit. (c) Which constraints bind? (d) The value of one extra machine-hour. (e) The value of raising the Q demand cap by one unit.

#### W11D1-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 2 — Technical diagnosis (≈ 55 min)

#### W11D2-A1 · WM · 4 pts
**Cover-and-reveal (bit operations).** Start with `x = 13` (binary `1101`). Apply in order: `x ← x XOR (x shifted right by 1)`; `x ← x AND 6`; `x ← (x shifted left by 3) OR 5`. (a) `x` after step 1. (b) after step 2. (c) after step 3 (decimal). (d) How many bits are set in the final `x`?

#### W11D2-B1 · IR · 6 pts ★
**Capacity failure (12 min).** *A service runs on 4 identical servers; each can process 100 requests/s (service rate μ = 100). Traffic is 260 requests/s, split evenly. Treat each server as a single queue with mean time in system 1/(μ − λ), where λ is that server's arrival rate.* (a) Per-server utilisation before and after **one** server fails. (b) Mean response time before and after (ms). (c) What happens if a **second** server fails? (d) One operational action you would take *first*, and why.

#### W11D2-C1 · HT · 5 pts
**Intermittent bug (10 min).** *A test fails 3 times in 200 runs on machine A and 0 times in 200 runs on machine B.* (a) If the true failure rate were 1%, the probability of **zero** failures in 200 runs. (b) The "rule of three" upper bound on the failure rate after 0 failures in 200 runs. (c) If failures were independent of the machine, the probability that all 3 failures out of the 3 observed in 400 runs land on machine A. (d) State your conclusion and the next experiment.

#### W11D2-D1 · HT · 4 pts
**Novel — fault localisation (8 min).** *Five components (1–5). Each test exercises a set of components: if **any** exercised component is faulty the test fails; otherwise it passes. Results: T1 exercises {1, 2, 3} → **fail**; T2 {3, 4, 5} → **fail**; T3 {1, 4} → **pass**; T4 {2, 5} → **pass**.* (a) Which components are certainly good? (b) Which component is faulty? (c) Suppose T3 had instead **failed** (T1, T2, T4 as before). List every smallest set of faulty components consistent with the results. (d) In (c), give one additional test that distinguishes among your candidate sets.

#### W11D2-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 3 — Statistical interpretation (≈ 55 min)

#### W11D3-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** (a) 95% confidence interval for a mean: `n = 100`, sample mean 50, standard deviation 10 (use 1.96). (b) By what factor must `n` grow to halve the interval's width? (c) `P(|Z| > 2)` for a standard normal (nearest 0.1%). (d) 20 independent tests at α = 0.05, all null: probability of at least one false positive.

#### W11D3-B1 · HT · 6 pts ★
**Regression to the mean (12 min).** *Two test scores, same population: mean 60, standard deviation 10, correlation 0.5. Students in the **top** decile on test 1 have a mean of 77.5 on test 1.* (a) Predict their mean on test 2 (no intervention). (b) Students in the **bottom** decile of test 1 (mean 42.5) then attend a tutoring programme. What test-2 mean would you expect **without** the programme, and how large an "improvement" is that? (c) Their actual test-2 mean is 51.5. What can (and can't) you conclude? (d) Design a fair evaluation.

#### W11D3-C1 · HT · 5 pts
**Sample size and peeking (10 min).** *An A/B test on a 10% baseline conversion rate wants to detect a 1-percentage-point lift. Use n per arm ≈ 16 p (1 − p) / d².* (a) n per arm. (b) With only a quarter of that sample per arm, what is the smallest lift detectable? (c) A team checks results daily for 14 days and stops the first time p < 0.05. What happens to the false-positive rate, and why? (d) Give a rule that avoids the problem.

#### W11D3-D1 · IR · 4 pts
**Subgroup mining (8 min).** *A drug trial finds no overall effect (p = 0.40). Analysts then examine 12 subgroups; women aged 40–50 show p = 0.03.* (a) Expected number of subgroups with p < 0.05 if the drug does nothing. (b) Probability of at least one such subgroup. (c) The Bonferroni threshold for each subgroup. (d) What would make the subgroup claim credible?

#### W11D3-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 4 — Algorithmic reasoning (≈ 55 min)

#### W11D4-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** (a) Maximum comparisons for binary search in a sorted list of 1,000 items. (b) An `O(n²)` algorithm takes 2 s at `n = 1,000`. Estimated time at `n = 5,000`. (c) Minimum comparisons that *any* comparison sort needs, in the worst case, for 8 items. (d) Edges in a tree with 50 nodes.

#### W11D4-B1 · AB · 6 pts ★
**Coin change (12 min).** *Coins: 1, 5, 12.* (a) The greedy method (always take the largest coin that fits) for 16: how many coins? (b) The true minimum for 16. (c) The smallest amount for which greedy is **not** optimal. (d) In how many distinct ways (unordered) can 12 be made? (e) Explain in a sentence why greedy fails here but works for coins 1, 5, 10. *(1 pt each for (a)–(d); 2 pts for (e).)*

#### W11D4-C1 · WM · 4 pts
**Recursion trace (cover-and-reveal).** `g(n)`: if `n ≤ 1` return `n`; if `n` is even return `g(n/2) + 1`; otherwise return `g(n − 1) + g(n − 2)`. (a) `g(3)` (b) `g(5)` (c) `g(9)` (d) How many **distinct** arguments are evaluated during `g(9)`?

#### W11D4-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Novel — project scheduling (10 min).** *Tasks (days): A (3), B (2, after A), C (4, after A), D (3, after B), E (2, after C **and** D).* (a) Minimum project duration with unlimited workers. (b) The critical path. (c) The slack of C. (d) Total duration with a single worker. (e) The project duration if D could be shortened by 2 days.

#### W11D4-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 5 — Causal inference (≈ 55 min)

#### W11D5-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** *A complication occurs in 12% of patients on treatment and 8% on control.* (a) Relative risk. (b) Absolute risk difference (percentage points). (c) Number needed to harm (patients per one extra complication). (d) Odds ratio (2 decimals).

#### W11D5-B1 · HT · 6 pts ★
**Difference in differences (12 min).** *A city (treated) launched a loyalty scheme; a comparison city (control) did not. Sales index: treated city 100 → 118; control city 100 → 108 (before → after).* (a) The naive before–after estimate. (b) The difference-in-differences estimate. (c) The identifying assumption. (d) *Pre-scheme data show the treated city normally grows 2.5 times as fast as the control city.* Recompute the estimate under this assumption. (e) What does the sign change in (d) teach about the method?

#### W11D5-C1 · HT · 5 pts
**Regression discontinuity (10 min).** *A scholarship is awarded to students scoring ≥ 70 on an entrance exam. Students scoring 70–72 earn 5% more, on average, ten years later than students scoring 68–69.* (a) Why compare a narrow band? (b) What could invalidate the design (name one)? (c) What effect does the estimate identify, and for whom? (d) Why can't you extend the estimate to students scoring 95?

#### W11D5-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Causal graph (10 min).** *Z causes both X and Y. X causes M; M causes Y. X also causes C and Y also causes C. You want the **total** effect of X on Y.* (a) Name the back-door path from X to Y. (b) Which variable(s) should you adjust for? (c) What goes wrong if you also adjust for M? (d) What goes wrong if you adjust for C? (e) If Z is unobserved, name **one** strategy that could still identify the effect.

#### W11D5-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 6 — Weekly challenge: mixed exam (≈ 75 min, one sitting)

*Twelve items, no domain order, one pass then one skip-return pass. Mark confidence 1–5. Apply the speed protocol.*

#### W11D6-1 · PD · 2 pts ★
`0, 1, 3, 8, 21, 55, ?` (a) The next term. (b) Give two different rules that generate the given terms.

#### W11D6-2 · AB · 3 pts ★
*The numbers 1, 2, …, 10 are on a board. Repeatedly erase two numbers a and b and write `a·b + a + b`. After nine steps one number remains.* (a) Find it. (b) Name the invariant that makes the answer independent of the order.

#### W11D6-3 · WM · 3 pts ★
**Cover-and-reveal.** Cards `A B C D E` (positions 1–5). (i) Swap positions 1 and 3. (ii) Rotate the whole row left by 2 (the first two cards go to the end). (iii) Reverse positions 2–4. Report the final order.

#### W11D6-4 · HT · 4 pts
*Three coins, one chosen at random: A fair, B lands heads 70%, C lands heads 90%. The chosen coin lands heads three times in a row.* (a) Posterior probability that it is C. (b) Probability the next flip is heads.

#### W11D6-5 · IR · 4 pts ★
*A restaurant has fixed costs of 60,000/month. Each cook costs 20,000/month and can prepare 1,500 meals; contribution is 40 per meal.* (a) Best number of cooks and monthly profit if demand is 4,000 meals. (b) Same if demand is 3,100 meals.

#### W11D6-6 · PE · 6 pts
**Sprint (4 min).** (a) `17²` (b) `C(7,3)` (c) `2¹²` (d) 15% of 240 (e) `3⁵` (f) `1/6 + 1/3`.

#### W11D6-7 · AB · 3 pts
*Is there a simple graph with these degree sequences?* (i) `4, 4, 3, 3, 2, 2` (ii) `5, 5, 3, 2, 2, 1`. Give a construction or an impossibility argument for each.

#### W11D6-8 · HT · 4 pts
*A hospital's 30-day readmission rate fell from 15% to 12% after a new discharge protocol; the hospital also changed how a "readmission" is recorded.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *definition-change* hypothesis and a *case-mix* hypothesis.

#### W11D6-9 · IR · 3 pts
(a) Expected draws to collect all 4 different coupons if each draw is uniform among 4. (b) Expected rolls of a fair die to see all six faces. (c) State the idea behind the calculation.

#### W11D6-10 · HT · 3 pts
*Three people: A says "B is a knave." B says "A and C are of the same type." C says "A is a knave." Knights always tell the truth; knaves always lie.* Determine each type and justify.

#### W11D6-11 · IR · 3 pts 🪤
*A bat and a ball cost 110 together. The bat costs 100 more than the ball.* (a) The ball's price. (b) *"5 machines make 5 widgets in 5 minutes."* How long do 100 machines take to make 100 widgets?

#### W11D6-12 · HT · 4 pts
*A colleague states: "Our new onboarding email raised activation, because activation rose from 30% to 36% after we sent it."* Write **four** distinct pieces of evidence that would change your mind, each with the direction it would push.

## Weekly reflection — Week 11

1. Which domain (business, diagnosis, statistics, algorithms, causal) cost me the most points, and was the loss in *setup* or *calculation*?
2. Did I write the representation before computing? What happened on the items where I did not?
3. Which conclusions did I write "what would change my mind" for, and did the exercise change any answer?
4. Which items tempted me to trust a familiar formula without checking its assumptions?
5. Where did ambiguity (definitions, units, missing assumptions) cost me time?
6. Confidence: which of my 5-rated answers were wrong?
7. **Strategy change for Week 12:** ________________________

**Calibration:** rated 5 → ____ / ____ correct; rated 1–2 → ____ / ____ correct.

# WEEK 12 — Integrated Reasoning II and Consolidation

**Phase:** Integration and dress rehearsal · **Difficulty:** 9+/10 · **Time pressure:** exam conditions on Days 5–6

### Cognitive objective

Treat every problem as unfamiliar. You will (1) reason through hard pattern and abstraction problems without a template, (2) combine business, technical and statistical reasoning in multi-variable cases, (3) sit a full mixed mock exam under time pressure, and (4) finish with a capstone of five long integrated problems. Compare this week with Week 1: the same five skills, but the setup is buried, the numbers interact, and there is rarely a single "obvious" method.

**Exam-mode rules (Days 5–6).** One pass in order, skip after 60–75 s, return to skipped items; write confidence 1–5 beside every answer; after the clock, do the untimed pass (mark `U`). The accuracy gate applies. Do not look anything up.

**Rule for every conclusion:** state *what evidence would change my mind*.

---

## Day 1 — Hard patterns and abstraction (≈ 55 min)

#### W12D1-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** (a) `2²⁰ mod 7` (b) `C(10,5)` (c) The digit sum of `2¹⁰` (d) The 100th digit after the decimal point of `1/7`.

#### W12D1-B1 · PD · 6 pts ★
**Hard sequences (10 min).** Next term.
(a) `1, 2, 4, 7, 12, 20, 33, ?`
(b) `2, 12, 36, 80, 150, ?`
(c) `1, 11, 21, 1211, 111221, 312211, ?`
(d) `3, 3, 5, 4, 4, 3, 5, 5, 4, ?`
(e) For each of (a)–(d): could a solver with **no prior knowledge of the sequence family** infer it from the terms alone? What would be needed? *(1 pt each for (a)–(d); 2 pts for (e).)*

#### W12D1-C1 · AB · 6 pts ★
**A two-move machine (12 min).** *A machine holds a pair (a, b) of positive integers. Start at (1, 1). Move X replaces (a, b) by (a + b, b). Move Y replaces (a, b) by (a, a + b).* (a) Can (5, 7) be reached? (b) Can (6, 9) be reached? (c) The minimum number of moves to reach (5, 7). (d) State and justify an invariant that decides reachability. (e) How many different pairs (a, b) with a + b = 10 are reachable? *(1, 1, 1, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D1-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Novel — optimal stopping (10 min).** *You roll a fair die. After seeing a roll you may keep it (your score) or roll again.* (a) The expected score if you get **two** rolls in total (and use the best policy). (b) The same with **three** rolls in total. (c) In the three-roll game, which first rolls do you keep? (d) Now suppose you may roll as often as you like, but every *extra* roll (after the first) costs 0.5. What is the expected net score under the best policy (score − 0.5 × number of extra rolls)? *(1, 1, 1, 2 pts.)*

#### W12D1-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 2 — Business and technical cases (≈ 55 min)

#### W12D2-A1 · WM · 4 pts
**Cover-and-reveal (ledger).** Accounts A = 100, B = 50, C = 0. In order: (i) move 20% of A to B; (ii) move half of B's balance to C; (iii) C sends 10 to A; (iv) B earns 10% interest on its balance. (a) A (b) B (c) C (d) The total of all three.

#### W12D2-B1 · IR · 6 pts ★
**Overbooking (12 min).** *A flight has 100 seats. Each ticket sells for 200. Each ticket holder independently shows up with probability 0.9. Every passenger who shows up but has no seat is bumped at a cost of 500 (on top of keeping the fare). You decide how many tickets n to sell.* (a) Expected profit if n = 100. (b) The best n (whole number) and its expected profit (use a calculator or a script; the binomial distribution is exact). (c) State the marginal rule: sell one more ticket if …. (d) Why is the optimum well above 100 even though bumping is expensive? *(1, 2, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D2-C1 · HT · 6 pts ★
**Latency after a release (12 min).** *A web service serves each request from cache (2 ms) or, on a miss, from the database (50 ms). Before the release the cache hit rate was 95%; after it, 80%. Database CPU rose 40%.* (a) Mean latency before and after. (b) The ratio of database query volume after to before. (c) If the extra query volume came entirely from cache misses and CPU is proportional to query volume, what fraction of the *baseline* database CPU was due to cache-miss queries? (d) Give **three** hypotheses for the drop in hit rate and one discriminating check for each. *(2, 1, 1, 2 pts.)*

#### W12D2-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Novel — newsvendor (10 min).** *A shop buys an item at 6 and sells it at 10. Unsold items are salvaged at 2. Demand is uniform on 0–100 (continuous).* (a) The critical ratio and the profit-maximising order quantity. (b) The expected profit at that quantity. (c) The optimal quantity if salvage were 4. (d) Explain why the optimum is where "cost of ordering one too few = cost of ordering one too many" in expectation. *(2, 1, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D2-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 3 — Statistics and causal inference (≈ 55 min)

#### W12D3-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** *A screening test: prevalence 2%, sensitivity 90%, specificity 95%.* (a) Positive predictive value. (b) Negative predictive value. (c) The positive likelihood ratio. (d) Expected false positives among 10,000 people screened.

#### W12D3-B1 · HT · 6 pts ★
**Database trawl (12 min).** *A DNA profile from a crime scene has a random-match probability of 1 in 1,000,000. Police search a database of 5,000,000 profiles and find one match.* (a) Expected number of coincidental matches. (b) If the true culprit is certainly in the database, the probability that the matched person is the culprit. (c) If there is only a 50% chance the culprit is in the database, that probability. (d) What additional evidence would change the conclusion? *(1, 2, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D3-C1 · HT · 6 pts ★
**Encouragement design (12 min).** *An online course was randomly promoted to half the users. Among promoted users, 60% took the course; among unpromoted, 20% took it anyway. Mean outcome: promoted 55, unpromoted 50.* (a) The intention-to-treat effect. (b) The difference in take-up. (c) The effect of taking the course among users whose take-up was changed by the promotion. (d) The assumptions needed. (e) Why is "takers vs. non-takers" a poor comparison? *(1, 1, 1, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D3-D1 · IR · 4 pts
**Forecast quality (8 min).** *Five forecasts (probability of "yes") and outcomes: 0.9 → yes; 0.8 → no; 0.6 → yes; 0.3 → no; 0.1 → no.* (a) The Brier score. (b) The Brier score of a forecaster who always says 0.4 (the observed frequency). (c) The skill score `1 − (Brier ÷ reference Brier)` against that reference. (d) Can five forecasts show that a forecaster is well calibrated? Explain.

#### W12D3-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 4 — Algorithms and constraints (≈ 55 min)

#### W12D4-A1 · PE · 4 pts
**Warm-up (4 min).** (a) Binary strings of length 8 with no two adjacent 1s. (b) Ways to tile a 2 × 5 rectangle with 2 × 1 dominoes. (c) Number of digits in `2³⁰`. (d) Permutations of 5 objects with no object in its original position.

#### W12D4-B1 · AB · 6 pts ★
**Knapsack (12 min).** *Capacity 10. Items (weight, value): A (6, 30), B (5, 20), C (5, 20).* (a) The result of the "best value-per-weight first" greedy method. (b) The true optimum. (c) The optimum if items may be **split** (fractional). (d) Use (c) to explain what gap you should expect between fractional and whole-item optima. (e) How many subsets of the three items fit? *(1, 1, 2, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D4-C1 · WM · 5 pts
**String rewriting (cover-and-reveal).** *Start with `A`. Each step rewrites every `A` as `AB` and every `B` as `A` (simultaneously).* (a) The string after step 5. (b) The number of `B`s after step 6. (c) The number of `A`s after step 6. (d) The 20th character of the string after enough steps (the limit word). *(2, 1, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D4-D1 · IR · 5 pts
**Novel — two machines (10 min).** *Jobs of lengths 5, 5, 4, 4, 3, 3, 3 must run on two identical machines; minimise the finishing time.* (a) A lower bound. (b) The result of "longest job first onto the currently least-loaded machine". (c) An assignment achieving the true optimum. (d) Why does the greedy method not reach the optimum here? *(1, 1, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D4-E · Error analysis (10 min)

---

## Day 5 — Timed mock exam (60 min + 10 min error analysis)

*Thirteen items, mixed skills. Exam-mode rules. Record confidence 1–5 for each.*

#### W12D5-M1 · PD · 2 pts
`1, 4, 10, 20, 35, 56, ?`

#### W12D5-M2 · AB · 3 pts
*Five lamps in a row, all off. Pressing lamp i toggles lamp i and its immediate neighbours (lamps at the ends have one neighbour).* (a) Can you switch all five on? (b) The minimum number of presses. (c) How many different sets of lamps to press achieve it (each lamp pressed at most once)?

#### W12D5-M3 · WM · 3 pts
**Stack machine.** `PUSH 3`, `PUSH 4`, `ADD`, `DUP`, `MUL`, `PUSH 5`, `SWAP`, `SUB`. (`SWAP` exchanges the top two items; `SUB` pops *b* (top) then *a* and pushes *a − b*; `DUP` copies the top.) Report the final stack.

#### W12D5-M4 · HT · 4 pts
*A condition has prior 1%. Test A (LR⁺ = 20) is positive; test B (LR⁻ = 0.2) is negative. Assume independence given the condition.* (a) Posterior odds. (b) Posterior probability. (c) One reason the independence assumption might fail.

#### W12D5-M5 · HT · 3 pts
*Rule: "If a card has a vowel on one side, it has an even number on the other." Visible faces: `E`, `K`, `4`, `7`.* Which cards must you turn over to test the rule, and why?

#### W12D5-M6 · AB · 3 pts
How many four-digit numbers (1000–9999) have a digit sum of exactly 10?

#### W12D5-M7 · HT · 3 pts
*A poll of 400 people finds 52% support.* (a) The 95% margin of error. (b) Can you conclude that a majority supports the proposal? (c) The sample size for a ±2 percentage-point margin (use p = 0.5).

#### W12D5-M8 · PE · 4 pts
**Sprint.** (a) `25 × 36` (b) `2¹⁵` (c) `0.375` as a fraction (d) `√1764`.

#### W12D5-M9 · HT · 4 pts
*A company's survey shows that teams using its new collaboration tool report higher satisfaction.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *selection* hypothesis and a *reverse-causation* hypothesis.

#### W12D5-M10 · IR · 3 pts 🪤
*Four doors, one prize. You pick door 1. The host, who knows where the prize is, opens two other doors showing no prize.* Probability of winning if you **switch** to the remaining door; if you **stay**.

#### W12D5-M11 · AB · 3 pts
(a) Is this true: *if `n²` is divisible by 12, then `n` is divisible by 6*? Justify. (b) Is this true: *…then `n` is divisible by 12*? Justify.

#### W12D5-M12 · WM · 3 pts
*S is taller than P; P is taller than Q; R is shorter than Q; T is shorter than R; U is taller than P and shorter than S.* (a) Who is the third-tallest? (b) How many people are taller than R? (c) Who is the shortest?

#### W12D5-M13 · IR · 4 pts
**Bet sizing.** *You repeatedly bet a fraction f of your wealth on an even-money bet that wins with probability 0.6.* (a) The fraction maximising long-run growth (Kelly fraction). (b) The expected log-growth per bet at that fraction (nearest 0.1%). (c) The expected log-growth at f = 0.6. (d) What does (c) show?

#### W12D5-E · Error analysis (10 min) — use the full eight-question page (§0.4).

---

## Day 6 — Capstone (≈ 90 min, one sitting)

*Five long problems. Write the representation first. Confidence 1–5 on each part.*

#### W12D6-1 · IR · 8 pts
**Retention programme.** *A company has 20,000 customers. 10% will churn next quarter. A model flags the 2,000 customers with the highest predicted risk; it catches 60% of all future churners. A retention offer costs 20 per flagged customer and prevents the churn of 30% of the flagged customers who would otherwise churn. A churner is worth 400.* (a) How many flagged customers are true churners? Precision and lift over the base rate? (b) Net benefit of the programme. (c) The smallest prevention rate at which the programme breaks even. (d) Suppose the offer irritates non-churners: 5% of flagged non-churners now churn. Recompute the net benefit. (e) Give two reasons the 30% prevention rate might not hold in practice. *(2, 2, 1, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D6-2 · AB · 5 pts
**Josephus.** *41 people stand in a circle numbered 1–41. Starting at 1, every third person still in the circle is eliminated, continuing round the circle.* (a) Who survives? (b) With 7 people (same rule), who survives? (c) Derive the recurrence you used. *(2, 1, 2 pts.)*

#### W12D6-3 · HT · 5 pts
**Sample-ratio mismatch.** *An experiment was designed to split users 50/50. It reports 10,300 users in A and 9,700 in B.* (a) Expected counts and the χ² statistic (one degree of freedom). (b) What does this suggest, and how strongly? (c) Name two mechanisms that could cause it. (d) What do you do with the reported treatment effect? *(2, 1, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### W12D6-4 · HT · 4 pts
**The 2-4-6 problem.** *A hidden rule accepts some triples of numbers; `2, 4, 6` is accepted. A learner tests `4, 6, 8`, `10, 20, 30` and `1, 3, 5`, gets "accepted" each time, and declares the rule "add 2 each time".* (a) Why is the conclusion unjustified? (b) List **three** tests that could falsify it, and say what each result would teach. (c) State a rule consistent with all four accepted triples that is not "add 2".

#### W12D6-5 · IR · 5 pts
**Screening at scale.** *One passenger in 10,000,000 is a genuine threat. A scanner detects 99.9% of threats and falsely alarms on 0.5% of non-threats. 100 million passengers pass each year.* (a) Expected number of genuine threats and of alarms. (b) The probability that an alarm is a genuine threat. (c) If a second, independent scanner with the same characteristics is applied to alarms only, the probability that a doubly-alarmed passenger is a genuine threat. (d) The design lesson. *(1, 2, 1, 1 pts.)*

# PART 3 — FINAL EXAMINATION

**Time:** 85 minutes of work (hard limit 90). **Questions:** 32. **Points:** 80.
**Rules:** paper and pencil only; no calculator, no internet, no notes, no answer key. One pass in order; skip an item after 60–75 seconds and mark it `↷`; return to skipped items with the time left. Write a **confidence 1–5** beside every answer. After the clock, do the **untimed pass** in a different colour (mark `U`), then mark the exam against Part 4.

**Suggested time plan.**

| Section | Skill | Items | Points | Minutes |

| | **Total** | **32** | **80** | **85** |

**Scoring conventions.** Objective items: full points for the correct answer with valid reasoning (where reasoning is requested); half credit is *not* available for correct answers with invalid reasoning on items marked "justify". Open-ended items use the rubric in Part 4. Every point is 1 pt unless the item says otherwise.

## Section B — Rule abstraction (10 pts, ≈ 15 min)

#### F-B1 · AB · 2 pts
*Nine coins lie in a row, all heads up.* (a) A move flips **two adjacent** coins. Can you reach all tails? Justify. (b) A move flips **three consecutive** coins. Can you reach all tails? If so, give the minimum number of moves and justify that it is minimal.

#### F-B2 · AB · 2 pts
(a) What is the smallest `n` such that **any** `n` integers contain two whose difference is divisible by 7? (b) What is the smallest `k` such that **any** `k` distinct numbers from 1–18 contain two that sum to 19? Justify both.

#### F-B3 · AB · 2 pts
For each statement, say true or false, and prove it or give a counterexample. (a) If `a × b` is even, then `a` and `b` are both even. (b) If `a + b` is odd, then exactly one of `a`, `b` is odd.

#### F-B4 · AB · 3 pts
*Every pair among 6 people are either friends or strangers.* (a) Show that there must be three mutual friends or three mutual strangers. (b) Show that with **5** people this can fail. *(2 pts and 1 pt.)*

#### F-B5 · AB · 1 pts
How many integers from 1 to 30 are divisible by **exactly one** of 3 and 5?

## Section C — Working memory in reasoning (13 pts, ≈ 15 min)

*Cover-and-reveal applies: read each item once, cover it, and work from memory of the setup; reveal only to check your understanding.*

#### F-C1 · WM · 3 pts
`x = 2, y = 3, z = 5`. In order: `x ← y + z`; `y ← x − z`; `z ← x × y`; `x ← z − y`. Report `x + y + z`.

#### F-C2 · WM · 3 pts
**Code-breaking.** *A secret code has three different digits from 1–6.* Guess `123` → no digit in the right place, one digit in the code but in the wrong place. Guess `145` → no digit in the right place, two digits in the code (wrong places). Guess `364` → no digit in the right place, two digits in the code (wrong places). Find the code and show that it is unique.

#### F-C3 · WM · 2 pts
Evaluate mentally: `((3 + 4) × (5 − 2)) − ((6 ÷ 2) + (8 − 5) × 2)`.

#### F-C4 · WM · 3 pts
*Five runners A–E finish in distinct positions. A finished before B. C finished immediately after D. A did not finish first. E finished between C and A (in position, not necessarily adjacent).* Give the finishing order and show it is unique.

#### F-C5 · WM · 2 pts 🪤
*A number is doubled, then 3 is subtracted, then the result is squared, then 7 is subtracted, then it is halved. The final result is 21.* Find **all** possible original numbers.

## Section D — Probability and hypothesis testing (17 pts, ≈ 15 min)

#### F-D1 · HT · 4 pts
*Machine M1 makes 60% of a factory's items, with a 2% defect rate; M2 makes 40%, with 5%. Defects are independent given the machine.* (a) An item is defective. Probability it came from M2. (b) Two items from the **same** unknown machine are both defective. Probability that machine is M2. (c) Probability that a third item from that machine is defective. *(1, 2, 1 pts.)*

#### F-D2 · HT · 3 pts
*Two cards are dealt from a standard 52-card deck.* (a) Probability both are aces, **given at least one is an ace**. (b) Probability both are aces, **given one of them is the ace of spades**. (c) Explain why the answers differ. *(1, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### F-D3 · HT · 4 pts
*A software company finds that users who use feature F retain twice as well as users who do not. The CEO proposes forcing all users through F.* Six-step template (§0.6). Include a *selection* hypothesis and a *dose* consideration, and say what evidence would change your mind.

#### F-D4 · HT · 3 pts
*You suspect a coin lands heads 60% of the time rather than 50%. In 10 flips you see 7 heads.* (a) The likelihood ratio (biased : fair). (b) The posterior probability that the coin is biased, starting from 50 : 50. (c) Is this strong evidence? Explain in one or two sentences. *(1, 1, 1 pts.)*

#### F-D5 · HT · 3 pts
*Two treatments X and Y. Group 1: X cured 8 of 10, Y cured 70 of 100. Group 2: X cured 30 of 100, Y cured 2 of 10.* (a) Which treatment is better within each group? (b) Which is better overall? (c) What determines which comparison to trust? *(1, 1, 1 pts.)*
